It just feels like a continuous struggle every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you normally enjoy, or have you been able to find pleasure in things despite feeling low and anxious?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest, as I haven't thought much about my hobbies lately. I've just been so overwhelmed by feeling constantly low and anxious that I haven't really tried to do the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless, or have you been experiencing any thoughts that you're a burden to others or that you'd be better off not being around?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel guilty or worthless, and I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or not wanting to be here. I've just been feeling incredibly down, tearful, and hopeless lately. It's like I'm constantly overwhelmed by sadness and can't find any way out of it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this persistent feeling of sadness and hopelessness, and has it been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it definitely hasn't been going on for two years. These feelings of sadness and hopelessness actually started recently, about a month ago. It all began right after a major stressful event in my life, and it has been pretty constant since then.\n\nDoctor: Is the major stressful event that triggered these feelings something that you think you should be able to cope with by now, or do you feel like the stress of the event is still actively affecting your life and contributing to your low mood and anxiety?\n\nPatient: The stress of that event is definitely still actively affecting my life and is the main reason I'm feeling so low and anxious. Since it's only been about a month, the impact is still very fresh and ongoing. I just feel completely unable to cope with it on my own right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble functioning in your daily life, such as with work, school, or relationships, due to these feelings of low mood and anxiety, or are you still able to manage your responsibilities despite the challenges you're facing?"
  }

Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\n- Answer only what the doctor asks.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been really struggling to cope lately after a major change in my life. I've been feeling a constant mix of low mood and anxiety, and it's making it really hard to get through the day. I just feel like I can't handle things right now.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about when these feelings of low mood and anxiety started, and have they been constant since the change in your life or have they been getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: They started about a month ago, just a couple of weeks after a major change in my life. Since then, the feelings of low mood and anxiety have been pretty constant and haven't really let up. It just feels like a continuous struggle every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you normally enjoy, or have you been able to find pleasure in things despite feeling low and anxious?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest, as I haven't thought much about my hobbies lately. I've just been so overwhelmed by feeling constantly low and anxious that I haven't really tried to do the things I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless, or have you been experiencing any thoughts that you're a burden to others or that you'd be better off not being around?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel guilty or worthless, and I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or not wanting to be here. I've just been feeling incredibly down, tearful, and hopeless lately. It's like I'm constantly overwhelmed by sadness and can't find any way out of it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this persistent feeling of sadness and hopelessness, and has it been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two years?\n\nPatient: Oh no, it definitely hasn't been going on for two years. These feelings of sadness and hopelessness actually started recently, about a month ago. It all began right after a major stressful event in my life, and it has been pretty constant since then.\n\nDoctor: Is the major stressful event that triggered these feelings something that you think you should be able to cope with by now, or do you feel like the stress of the event is still actively affecting your life and contributing to your low mood and anxiety?\n\nPatient: The stress of that event is definitely still actively affecting my life and is the main reason I'm feeling so low and anxious. Since it's only been about a month, the impact is still very fresh and ongoing. I just feel completely unable to cope with it on my own right now.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble functioning in your daily life, such as with work, school, or relationships, due to these feelings of low mood and anxiety, or are you still able to manage your responsibilities despite the challenges you're facing?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure how to measure it, but I've just been trying my best to get by. It has felt increasingly difficult to keep up with everything lately, and I feel like I'm falling behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if the stress from the major change in your life were to be resolved or significantly reduced, your low mood and anxiety would also improve, or do you feel like these feelings have taken on a life of their own and would persist even if the external stressors were removed?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I definitely think they would improve if that stress was resolved. My low mood and anxiety are completely tied to this recent change, so if that pressure was lifted, I feel like I'd get back to normal. It doesn't feel like an independent, permanent issue that would stay without the stressor.

This temporal relationship between the stressful event and the onset of symptoms supports Adjustment Disorder. However, the presence of persistent feelings of sadness and hopelessness, which have been constant for about a month, also aligns with criteria for Major Depressive Disorder. Since the patient's symptoms have not been present for two years and are directly tied to the stressful event, Adjustment Disorder remains a strong candidate. Major Depressive Disorder is still considered due to the significant impact of the symptoms on the patient's daily life and the presence of feelings of hopelessness, but the direct link to the stressful event and the patient's belief that resolution of the stress would lead to improvement in symptoms keeps Adjustment Disorder as a primary consideration. Further evaluation is needed to determine the best diagnosis.", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
This conversation has reached a point where a diagnosis can be considered based on the information provided. Adjustment Disorder is characterized by the development of emotional or behavioral symptoms in response to an identifiable stressor, which occurs within 3 months of the onset of the stressor. The symptoms or behavior are clinically significant as evidenced by either of the following: marked distress that is out of proportion to the severity or intensity of the stressor, significant impairment in social or occupational (academic) functioning. In this case, the patient's symptoms seem to be directly tied to the recent major change in their life, and they express a belief that their low mood and anxiety would improve if the stress from this change was resolved. This aligns closely with the criteria for Adjustment Disorder. Major Depressive Disorder, on the other hand, involves one or more major depressive episodes in a person's lifetime, characterized by persistent patterns of sadness or a lack of interest in outside stimuli, which significantly impair functioning. While the patient exhibits some symptoms that could be indicative of a depressive disorder, such as low mood, anxiety, and hopelessness, the direct linkage of these symptoms to a specific stressor and the patient's belief in their potential for improvement with resolution of the stressor leans more towards Adjustment Disorder. 

Given the information and the criteria for these disorders, the most likely diagnosis appears to be Adjustment Disorder. However, a definitive diagnosis would require a comprehensive evaluation, including a thorough psychiatric history, assessment of current symptoms, and consideration of other potential diagnoses or comorbid conditions. 

For the purpose of this interview, the next step might involve discussing treatment options or coping strategies for Adjustment Disorder, but since the format requires a question:

Adjustment Disorder is characterized by the development of emotional or behavioral symptoms in response to an identifiable stressor, which occurs within 3 months of the onset of the stressor. The symptoms or behavior are clinically significant as evidenced by either of the following: marked distress that is out of proportion to the severity or intensity of the stressor, significant impairment in social or occupational (academic) functioning. In this case, the patient's symptoms seem to be directly tied to the recent major change in their life, and they express a belief that their low mood and anxiety would improve if the stress from this change was resolved. This aligns closely with the criteria for Adjustment Disorder. Major Depressive Disorder, on the other hand, involves one or more major depressive episodes in a person's lifetime, characterized by persistent patterns of sadness or a lack of interest in outside stimuli, which significantly impair functioning. While the patient exhibits some symptoms that could be indicative of a depressive disorder, such as low mood, anxiety, and hopelessness, the direct linkage of these symptoms to a specific stressor and the patient's belief in their potential for improvement with resolution of the stressor leans more towards Adjustment Disorder. \n\nGiven the information and the criteria for these disorders, the most likely diagnosis appears to be Adjustment Disorder. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI'm not quite sure how to measure it, but I've just been trying my best to get by. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.
